Um well if you put a simple rule or a simple constraint on that state space for example if you look at the number of consecutive leading zeros in the output of a SHA 256 hash beyond some threshold you can start to find interesting properties and you can use those properties in a distributed computer. So, here's an output, 256 bits. I've colored in the first nine leading zeros, and that's the that's what we're measuring. And we say in this space, 256 bits, the first nine in this example are zeros. Uh because there's there's not one global clock in this distributed computer. And till next time. Till next time. All right. Bye. Um for the third uh session of the third day of the symposium we have active inference in biology modeling the environment is an agent. Uh first Chris Fields will present topic and then Virginia Blue will facilitate the Q&A. Um thank you uh for presenting. The stage is yours please. >> Okay. Thank you very much. Uh I want to talk today about applications of active inference in biology and particularly the issue of modeling the environment as an active inference agent which the FEP in fact requires us to do. Um but the approach I want to take is via the symmetries of the FEP and I want to focus in particular on two symmetries of the FEP and hence the active inference formalism. One is that all components of any decomposition of any system are active inference agents and need to be treated as such. And the other symmetry is that all boundaries between systems, so all Markoff blankets for example in the classical picture are information symmetric. Both of these symmetries are universal. Uh they come just from the physics, but they're both especially relevant to thinking of active inference in a biological or social setting. So in in thinking about any interactions between living systems and their environments um these two symmetries have to be taken fairly seriously from a modeling methodology point of view. So where we're going today is first to discuss a little bit of physics and um I'll be using physics from the top down. So starting with the description of a of an entire isolated system, some kind of universe and adopting very simple assumptions and seeing where that physics takes us. And this is kind of an unfamiliar um way to start thinking about the physics of a system. If you're typically used to starting with particles uh behaving in spaceime or even a dynamical system that's defined in some spatial coordinates. Uh we won't in particular be talking about spatial coordinates at all. We'll be talking about systems completely independently of any spatial embedding. So once we're done with the physics, I want to consider two examples. Uh one is niche construction and show how niche construction is an instance of active inference. And then the other example will be cognitive offloading. taking part of your problem and giving it to your environment to solve for you. And I'll also show that that generically is an outcome of active inference. And then finally u I'll talk about some methodological implications of um this way of thinking and in particular methodological implications of these two symmetries of the FEP. So, um, top down physics, we're just going to assume three things. We're going to assume that any, um, system that we're interested in can be described with a finite Hilbert space. So, a Hilbert space is just a space whose basis vectors are the possible values of the possible degrees of freedom of the system. So if you think of asking a system a bunch of yes no questions every yes no question corresponds to one of the basis vectors of this state space if you use a hilbert spaces the state space and therefore we can think of any of these spaces as just spaces of cubits quantum bits u because those are the most fundamental entity to which you can ask a yes no question and get an answer either yes or no. So we'll assume that all systems can be described in this way and we'll assume that they're all finite dimensional and that's just for convenience. They can be arbitrarily large but if we assume they're finite dimensional we don't have to worry about singularities which just cause problems. The second assumption we'll make is that there's some sort of background time parameter. There's some um intuitive concept of time that we can use to talk about change. Uh and we will not assume that this is measurable by any experiment. So it's it's just a parameter that we'll use in the theory and measurable times have to be constructed but they're constructed from this back background time parameter and the way to think about that is a measurable time requires a clock and for a clock to be meaningful it has to change and to talk about change we have to assume some sort of background time. So all clocks run slower than the fundamental frequency of the background time parameter that we're assuming. Then the third assumption we're going to make is that isolated systems conserve information. And this is the principle of unitarity which is the fundamental principle of quantum theory. Um it says that if you have an isolated system and its state is evolving in this background time then the state will encode exactly the same amount of information at every time. Um that assumption might be considered controversial. uh if you throw out that assumption then you throw out quantum theory. So you've thrown out a lot of empirical physics and have a lot of explaining to do uh it requires a whole new theory of how everything works. Uh if you abandon unitarity so we're doing we're making these assumptions with a goal which is universality. If we make these assumptions, we describe all systems and all interactions um up to the three assumptions that we've made. Uh they apply to everything and of course we play pay a price for this which is abstraction. Um the results that we get will give us constraints on models but they don't tell us how to build models. So the outcome is is a set of methodological constraints that have to be complied with. It's not a model building procedure. And before um going on, I'll put in a a plug for this recent book that Jim Glazebrook and I wrote that explores the implications of making these assumptions. and we're doing a live stream on that at the end of January um for the active inference institute. So if you're interested in this kind of approach to physics um that may be of interest to you again it's not for a couple of months. So let's start with the first implication of unitarity which I label here decompositional equivalence. The implication is that you can take a system any isolated system. So I'll call that U for universe and that system has some internal dynamics which we can represent with a a Hamiltonian operator. You don't have to worry about how to write that down or anything else. Uh it's an operator that classically corresponds to the total energy of the system. But uh it is an operator that preserves information the information in the state of this isolated system U. And you being isolated just means that information doesn't flow in and it doesn't flow out. So decompositional equivalence is the fact that I can now slice that system any way I want to into two components which I'll call S and E for system and environment and I can represent each of those components as having an internal dynamics and I can represent their interaction uh by again a Hamiltonian operator that describes information flow between the two systems at some boundary the boundary where I've sliced the system and that operator HU decomposes additively which means that I can slice this system any way I want to and I don't affect its overall dynamics at all. So what that means methodologically is that physics by itself does not give us boundaries. They're no joints in you or for it to be carved at the boundaries that we see are all boundaries that we have defined by stipulation. So the picture of an evolving universe where the physics itself gives you the boundaries around things is actually inconsistent with the three assumptions that we've made. Uh the physics doesn't produce any boundaries if you make those three assumptions. However, the physics defines the interaction which I've labeled here hse at any boundary that we stipulate by making a model. So it's our choice as theorists. We can slice the system any way we want and the physics gives us the interaction and it gives it to us by this simple formula I've written here. So this symmetry in fact is key to the free energy principle. And now we can state the free energy principle. It says that if we slice this system U, if we slice any isolated system anywhere to define two components S and E, then if S and E are to remain in a separable joint state, so if they're going to remain mutually statistically independent, then they have to both behave in a way that's consistent with that mutual statistical independence, which in in quant Quantum theory we call separability. Separability just means lack of entanglement. So if S and E are going to be separate systems, things that we can talk about independently, they're not entangled. So they have to behave in a way that is consistent with them not being entangled. and maintaining separability not becoming entangled requires keeping the interaction weak with respect to the internal interactions. So we can express that by thinking of the dimensions of the interaction which are the same as the dimensions of the Hilbert spaces. the dimension of the interaction is the same as the dimension of this boundary that I've used to slice the system. And that boundary just has to be much smaller than either of the systems that it separates. Um, and that is intuitively obvious. The boundary has to be small enough that the systems robustly have internal states or the FE doesn't tell you anything. The FE requires that systems have internal states. But this gives us a an an important qualitative fact which is that sensations and actions which are defined at the boundary are lowdimensional projections of the general models of the systems because the general models are implemented by these internal interactions. So when we think about what a system is observing or what a system is doing in both cases those are very lowdimensional representations of its of its generative model lowdimensional inputs lowdimensional outputs. So with this we can now think about the symmetries of the FEP. So the first symmetry is that if E is the environment of S, then S is the environment of E. So if S's generative model, its internal dynamics, is predicting E's action on the boundary, then E's generative model, which is its internal dynamics, is predicting S's actions on the boundary. And E's actions on the boundary are S's perceptions or sensations. U S's actions on the boundary are E's perceptions or sensations. So the boundary is the locust of all of the sensations or actions of SNE. The boundary is the markoff blanket between them which encodes sensation and action in the standard formulation of the FE. So both of these systems are constantly acting to minimize variational free energy defined at the same boundary. So this minimization of VF is always a co-minimization being undertaken by both the system and its environment. uh and the reason is that both a system and its environment are stri trying to stay separate from each other. They're trying to keep the environment well- definfined. They're trying to keep the boundary well- definfined. And the only way to do that is to keep this interaction weak. It's to keep the the interaction from kind of blowing up in their faces and tearing the boundary apart. So that's the first symmetry of the FEP. If the system's an agent, the environment's an agent, too. The second symmetry is that this interaction being well defined at the boundary requires that the information exchange be symmetric. So, uh again, this interaction is an operator um that acts at the boundary to move information between S and E. And it's the same operator from whichever direction you look at it. And the operator being well- definfined is the same condition as the boundary being well defined. And in fact, we can see that intuitively by thinking of the boundary as a cubit space. Again, it's a finite dimensional Hbert space. And if we think of the boundary as a cubit space, it's just some set of cubits. And the system and the environment are acting on that cubit space using perfectly symmetrical uh sets of operators. Um sort of the the S half of HSC and the E half of HSC and each is doing preparation and measurement of each of those cubits. So the boundary being well defined, the interaction being well defined requires this symmetry and you can think of that just as Newton's third law u of equal and opposite response to any imposed force. So this again is true of everything. So now now let's think about what this means in a couple of examples. Uh first it means we can always think of active inference as niche construction. Uh S is always acting on E to persuade E to minimize S's variational free energy. Why? Because S's variational free energy is also E's variational free energy. They have the same boundary and they're acting on it with a symmetrical operator. So E acting on S is always trying to persuade S to minimize E's VF. So the two are always co-minimizing their VF. So niche construction is niche co-construction. S in fact is E's niche. Uh and E is constantly trying to get that niche i.e. s to behave itself to be to behave in a way that's as favorable to E as possible. So good niches in Mike Leven Mike Leven's language are persuadable niches. U they're niches that we can by acting on them get them to behave in a way that's beneficial. Uh and that includes of course the other organisms in the niche whether they're conspecifics or not. So a a niche of a social organism like a human is fairly persuadable. U niches of of kind of loners like um tigers for example are less persuadable. So the tiger is still acting on its niche but it's not very persuadable. So, um, it doesn't get as far as we typically do in getting our niche to behave. But the bottom line is that because active inference and niche construction are deeply the same thing, ecology is intrinsically a social science. It's intrinsically about persuasion. So, we're starting to cross the boundaries between disciplines now. So let's think about the other example which is cognitive offloading. Uh getting the environment to help solve some problem for you. So if if I'm an agent um and I'm acting on my environment by making a measurement and recording some result and by recording the result I'm making an action. Um, if I wait any finite amount of time, then my environment's going to have some time to do something with the information that I wrote on the boundary by taking my action uh on the environment by making the measurement. So the environment does something and if I go back and make another measurement, I'm going to see the results of what the environment has done in response to my first measurement. And generically, I'm going to get a different answer for my measurement because the environment's in a different state. So the environment is constantly processing the information that is written on it by observers. So it's not it's not just sitting there. It's never passive. And one particular case of this is memory. If I write memory on some memory on the environment, then in general the environment is going to process that memory and when I go to retrieve it, it's going to look just slightly different. And uh we know that that's in fact the case for episodic memories by humans and the environment is always acting on our brains and so it changes um our brain states and when you go to retrieve a memory um it's likely to be subtly different. Uh the same is clearly true with technology. We spend a lot of time and effort trying to get our technologies to to modify the memories, rewrite on them as little as possible. So what what does this mean? Uh what do these symmetries actually imply? Well, first it means that systems environments are always co-evolving in time. Separability, lack of entanglement means that the interaction between a system and its environment can be considered classical communication. They can be considered to be exchanging classical bitst strings. But because they're not entangled, because they're separable, they have free choice uh by the Conway cooken theorem or just as a consequence of separability. So the languages that system and environment are using to write on the boundary can be different and likely will be different. So even though the environment's giving me back a bit string, it may be a bit string that's been encoded with a reference frame that I don't know and I can't find out. I just have to make assumptions about it, build a model of it to try to understand what the environment's telling me. So the other implication is that um system and environment are constantly processing each other's informationational inputs and then giving back answers. So they're constantly trying to solve each other's problems. um whether the answer is actually interpretable or not is always an open question. So in terms of theoretical methodology and also experimental methodology, um if we make these physics-based assumptions, then the passive environment assumption is always wrong. And it may work for all practical purposes sometimes, but it's very difficult to say which ones those are. And in fact, the frame problem in artificial intelligence is a statement of the fact that we don't know uh when the passive environment assumption is actually going to work. We don't know whether the environment is going to generate side effects that we're not able to predict or haven't been able to predict. A second methodological assumption is that we have to assume history dependence. Um and in classical physics we often assume that things are not history dependent. Um that means they don't have internal states. Uh they're not doing information processing. And if systems really aren't doing any information processing, if they really don't have internal states, if they're really no larger than their boundaries, then they're not separable. Then they're in fact entangled with their environments. So if we think something's separable and it looks like it isn't history dependent, we just need to look harder because it's some level of description we're going to find history dependence. And then the final implication is that if a system is separable, if if we can talk about it as an independent thing, it's always going to be getting some extra cognitive resources from its environment. Uh, it's always going to be able to do some offloading of problem solving tasks, but it may not know how to use the answers and we not we may not be able to detect without doing the right kind of experiment what its environment is actually trying to tell it. So I just want to emphasize in the last slide that we really have only assumed three things here. We've just assumed some very basic physics uh the Hbert space description, background time and unitarity. And so we get a lot of biology and even cognitive science for free out of these very simple physical assumptions. And in fact, we get all the way to cognitive offloading for free just from the physics. And so when we're thinking about physical systems, which we always are when we're doing active inference sort of models, um we start with the physics and we get um cognition basically uh not just in the system but in the environment. So so this project started out of the backpack consortium which is uh intended to study chronic low pack low back pain and classify various different patients into groups that might respond to various different therapies. So basically what we do is we take their their their clinical trial data which is about 2,000 variables or so and we use language models to assemble that data into a narrative. Then we take that transcript, we process that again with actually 11 different uh self-hosted language models and we we kind of come up with a new one and then we have another meeting with them uh where they criticize that one. And so we do this two or three times and finally we get to a validated narrative that the patient actually says this this represents me. It represents my attitude. It represents the things I have to say about that. So the reason we're doing this is we have this working hypothesis that inside the human there's latent information that our objective measures just don't capture, right? Hi, I'm David and I'm going to talk briefly about bounded rationality and multi- aent alignment. So to begin with some motivation, a lot of focus in alignment research is centered on this question of how do we align a single potentially superhuman level AI with humans or humanity. The big issue here aside from how we define these things is that there's no agreed upon solution to this problem and it seemed like a very difficult one in general. On the other hand, a lot of focus in capabilities research centered on the question of how we build a single superhuman level AI system. The reality is that for such systems there are significant trade-offs between performance and cost. Moreover, AI agents are increasingly being deployed on the internet to carry out tasks autonomously over longer and longer horizons. Fundamentally, we existed and are building towards a multi-polar world which is an ecosystem of many AIs, humans and organizations all interacting with each other. And so a central question here is how do we ensure that this ecosystem is aligned, beneficial, efficient and resilient. The hypothesis I want to explore here is that we start from the fact that a major source of risk from advanced AI comes from this extreme uninterruptible optimization towards specific goals. Now if a significant proportion of the AI agents in this ecosystem are highly capable, have access to significant amounts of resources and are insufficiently aligned. This could create destabilizing undesirable outcomes. And so perhaps complimentary to working on alignment, maybe we should also think about limiting capabilities and resources within such ecosystems until and as we progress with alignment. So what this what could this lead to? Well, one of our essential hypotheses is that this could lead to an ecosystem of specialized, efficient, and reliable agents all working together alongside humans to solve problems much in the same way as we would see organizations or societies do. So, how might we reason about such systems? Well, one concept that we propose that's potentially useful is out of bounded rationality. So what is bound rationality? Well, the fundamental idea here is that real world decision-m is subject to limitations. These limitations can come in the form of limited time, energy, money, computational resources, attention, memory, information, etc. And crucially, decision-m need to take these factors into account if it is to be effective in the real world. Now, boundary rationality was originally intended as a descriptive model of human decision-m then was later expanded to encompass both biological and artificial decision makers as well as to bridge the gap between both normative and descriptive theories of decision-m. The key mechanism here is that of satisficing which is related to finding the optimal solution in a simplified model or finding a good enough solution in a more realistic model. And we see these kinds of trade-offs manifest in the modern day in the form of cost performance trade-offs especially with the rise of test time inference. So here we see several graphs from major AI companies, researchers and also public benchmarks that are increasingly focused on the trade-off between performance on a certain task or benchmark and the costs in terms of test time or training time compute or in terms of dollars for inference etc. So just for a brief timeline of bounded rationality, the concept really got started with Herbert Simon who introduced it in the 1950s and wrote a lot of seinal works on this. Then sort of the 1970s and 80s, it was later extended and really fleshed out by Amos Terski and Daniel Canaman in their research program on heristics and cognitive biases. as well as their distinction between system one and system two thinking. Then later in the 90s you have good gigarenzer who used concepts of an adaptive toolbox and ecological rationality as well as to Russell who worked on bounded optimality and you know since then especially in the 2000s up to now there's a proliferation of approaches and ways of modeling this some of which I'll talk about next. So one of the key concepts here is computational rationality and the basic idea is that we want to select the optimal program for some valid machine to execute. And so you can imagine in this diagram on the left here, you have some sort of utility function in an environment from which you select an optimal program within a space of feasible programs and you can study the optimal behavior for such programs and connected to observed organism behaviors. On the right here you see another way of thinking about this idea of computational rationality where you have your utility which is diminishing with a cost of delays. And this trade-off means that there's an optimal time for you to run your computation before you select an action. And a key concept here is that of meta level reasoning which focuses on focuses on considering the current uncertainties the time critical losses with continuing in computation and the expected gains in precision of reasoning with additional computation. Now another concept that's been increasingly popular in recent years is that of resource rationality. And the key idea here is that we were trying to find the optimal way for an agent to use its limited resources. So you can imagine this triangle here representing a space of possible lines that are implementable with our brain's limited resources and on the y- axis you have performance of these different lines. The resource rational approach is to find the optimal one within this feasible set. And some of the key steps associated with resource rational analysis is that first you define the problem at the computational level. Then you postulate a class of algorithms that the mind might use to approximately solve the problem. The cost of computational resources and the utility of accuracy of the algorithms. After that you want to find the algorithm in the class that best trades off resources and accuracy. Then you evaluate predictions of this algorithm against empirical data. And if there's a gap, you go back to step one or two. Now, another concept within this space is that of information theoretic under rationality. And the key idea here is that we account for the cost of cognition using tools and concepts of information theory. In particular, agents start with some prior probability distribution Q over choices and does some computation or information processing to transform it into a posterior P. And you can measure the cost of this transformation as the K divergence from the prior to posterior. And so agent optimizes an objective which is an expected utility minus a regularizer which represents the information. So few questions related to boundary rationality in alignment in this context of ecosystems is first of all how do we build these specialized efficient and reliable agents. Secondly, how do we coordinate such agents with each other and with humans or organizations to reliably and safely solve more complex problems? Finally, how do we mitigate the risk that might emerge critically in the context of these multiation systems? Now, our view is that this is an important research agenda and we hope that we are able to dedicate resources and talent towards solving these important problems. Thanks for listening. Hi. So, Mao and I are going to be uh presenting some thoughts and ideas that we have on path flexibility and collective alignment. So, to start off with some motivation, why path flexibility and what is it? Our starting point is essentially that approaches to alignment should address some key properties of the real world which include non-stationality of the environment, non-stationality and diversity of human beliefs and preferences, bounded rationality and imperfect execution of plans, collective and system level properties and also the embodiment embodiment and embeddedness of agents within their environment. And so to address some of these issues, we suggest that we focus on paths and processes. And these things would include the alignment of beliefs and preferences in terms of how they update and not just what they are. The ability to efficiently adapt and recover when environments or goals shift. and also the maintenance and flexibility and optionality over time rather than rigidity and narrowness. And so a simple example of this is a lava world example where agent has to go to its goal and traverse this pathway where there's lava on either side. And the basic intuition here is that the agent should expend a little bit of extra effort to traverse the middle path because this provides them more flexibility and resilience in case they accidentally slip um to either side. So some of the key objectives that we hope to achieve within our work. Firstly, to demonstrate the value of our alignment formalism. Secondly, to study how path flexibility and empowerment lead to resilience and finally to develop methods to promote path flexibility and empowerment. So some key concepts that we find useful to study and address and define. Well, first of all, there is past flexibility, which we refer to as the quantity and ease of shifting between viable trajectories for an agent or group. Then there is empowerment, which roughly relates to the degree of influence that an agent can exert on its environment. There's resilience which is the ability to recover from and adapt to adversity through mechanisms of redundancy and degeneracy. And there's concept of attractor difference which is roughly the distance between agents stable belief or preference trajectories. And the key idea here is that the closer aligned agents attractors are the more similar their trajectories will be. And there is plasticity which is roughly the degree to which beliefs, preferences or policies can update in light of prediction errors and changing circumstances. There is planning horizon which is also related to light cone. Uh which essentially measures the effective temporal depth and breadth of active inference or planning into the future. And finally, precision control, which is the capacity to modulate uncertainty uh including sensory policy or belief precision. So some key questions that we want to address. First of all, how do coordination and communication contribute towards path flexibility and systemwide resilience? Here we hypothesize that these things come hand in hand. Uh second question, how do do high attractive difference and low plasticity lead to lower collective path flexibility? This work is a collaboration between the institute for advanced consciousness studies mil and university of Montreal. Instead of chasing abstract AGI in the usual way, my work focuses on what I call social neuro AI and um brain inspired architecture. We take the brain as a modular pilot system and we use active inference idea to treat each module as an implicit uh model of the world and other agent. The big idea is that human compatible intelligence must be social from the start with architectural law prior that favor poor social behavior theory of mind and mutual modeling. Let me start with uh how complex social behavior emerge um and how has been studied today. Um in open AI hide and seek environment which was published in 2020. Selfplay create creates an autocurriculum of strategies like run chase build for use ram defend ram and so on. Um team learn um to use tools and division of labor but each agent is still uses a single monolithic policy network and the internal structure of these strategies is opaque and it's not interpretable. Roma take a a step toward a structure by explicit modeling rules. Each agent sample a role from its observation, condition its policy on the role and regularize the um by that and push roles to be identifiable, compact and specialized. This allowed them to in to have more interpretable division of rap labor but rules are still imposed in a top-down way rather than emerge directly from a modular architecture. Our core idea is that big monolithic network entangle everything. So any change becomes risky and unpredictable. If one part breaks, the whole system can lose its ability. In contrast, true collective intelligence from ants to human relies on diverse experts. Decentralization and knowing who needs to track whom. We want systems that look more like a team of a specialized mechanism that share only a sparse information. So adaptation stay local and robust. To get this kind of specialization, we built on recurrent independent mechanism or rims. Instead of one big recurrent neural network, we use several smaller recurrent modules and each module has its own hidden state and its own parameter. At each time step, we run an input attention phase. Every module send a query. The input gives keys and um values and top case selection decide which modules actually receive and process the information. Only those active modules update their hidden state through their recurrent cell. For example, a small GRU while the inactive one simply keep their previous memory. Then we run a communication attention phase where active module use query key and value over each other state. So they can exchange a small amount of information before going back to mostly independent dynamic. This gives us a sparse activation, a sparse communication and a natural way for u modules to be a specialized. Our first um maple architecture that we introduce here is the rim only model which is also our best performer. Each agent gets um an egocentric pixel observation for the policy and a global observation for the value which we pass through a normal CNN encoder. The encoder features then goes into a rim and the combined state is used to produce both policy and the value. Over time, different rim can specialize in tracking different aspect of the environment or other agent um giving us a modular social polic policy. We also use uh independent PPO as uh our multi- aent architecture base. The second architecture aim for a stronger interpretability and objectentric reasoning. Instead of a standard encoding, use a slot attention to factor the scene into a set of latent slot where each slot tend to represent an object or entity. We then feed each slot into its own recurrent module again a rim unit so that objects are tracked over time through dedicated memory pathway. This makes it easier to to say things like this module is tracking the opponent and this slot holds the share resource information. For this to work, the slot feature themselves must be high quality. We pre-train a slot encoder using reconstruction and a compositional consistency loss on data collected from the environment. So the same object is mapped to similar slot across different scene. The Tessani plot in the latent state of the slot here shows the initial result slots are starting to separate but clusters are messy and overlapping which push us to design a strandic a stronger inductive biases to sharpen these representation in the latent space and make them more cluster. We introduce our perceptual orthogonal projection loss or POPL. POPPL encourage different slot to be distinct. Push class center to be orthogonal and pull features towards their center. Different object type or rules should sit in orthogonal direction in feature space and each type should form a tight uh cluster. After adding POPL, the TES plus in the latent space should clean well separated cluster which gives our rim a much more disentangle objectentric input. So I love uh Avil's presentation and I have a you know I've have a lot of work that bears a close affinity to what he was talking about but today I'm going to talk about a new kind of new direction in which I'm taking active inference in my thinking uh and it's uh about um I think it provides the resources to uh critically interrogate some uh very uh old and classic assumptions about human thought and action. embedded in the European philosophical tradition and offer an alternative to them. So intuitively there is this distinction we make between patterns we find in nature. For example, laws of nature that science finds and patterns we make um uh cultural phenomena mainly music, architecture, dance, technology, uh language and so forth. And this distinction has a very old provenence in European philosophy. Aristotle made a distinction between theoria which uh is a uh capacity to discover patterns in the world and praises and poises which is about making patterns. Um and he even he even hypothesized two different cognitive processes. One theoretical the theoretical syllogism one the practical syllogism underlying these two capacities. This has been taken up in cont uh re more recent European philosophy in terms of the uh distinctions in direction of fit. So some cognitive acts and speech acts have a worldto-word direction of fit. What does that mean? That means if there's a discrepancy between what you say and the world, you alter the world in some cases. But in in other cases, there's a word to world direction of fit where you alter the word to uh respect the world. So uh Elizabeth Anskim has a very memorable illustration of this with the grocery list. Suppose you have a list um and you're doing your groceries. Um there the the word is d is regulating the world. So if you don't have what you want on the grocery list, if there's a discrepancy, you change the world in order to match the list. But you can imagine a private investigator following the person around and making an identical list. Um but there the direction of fit is in the opposite direction. The the list is modified in light of what the person puts in their shopping cart. Surl uh uh uh marked this distinction as the distinction between declarative and imperative speech acts. Some speech acts uh declaratives aim to track the world and if they don't track the world it's the speech act that has to be changed. Other uh speech acts like commands imperative speech act aim to change the world and if there's a discrepancy it's the world that has to change. Um this has been applied to models uh classical model cognitive models of the mind where there's a distinction between belief modules and desire modules. Uh Hume is very explicit about this distinction. Uh Peter Kurthers is a contemporary example where belief modules aim to track the world and desire modules produce states that aim to alter the world primarily uh the body. Now the interesting thing in uh active inference is um that the proposal is the brain doesn't work that way. Here's Andy Clark's uh very uh interesting recent article where he writes desires and motivations are fluently accommodated within the unifying predictive processing schema where they emerge as webs of prior beliefs that sculpt probabilistic predictions some of which become positions so as to bring about actions. Importantly, a single construct here plays the role of belief and desire. So, it's a departure from this classical dichconomy between uh states that have a word to world direction of fit and states that have a world-to-word direction of fit. Um, now what I find interesting about this is it can make sense of certain um uh um phenomena, linguistic and cognitive phenomena that don't fall neatly into either category. And one uh such example is the concept of ideology which has gotten expression in uh any number of thinkers including Marx, Fukco, Nichze and Freud. But basically what I want to suggest is a a kind of quick uh fast def definition of ideology is that it's pattern making and masquerading as pattern finding. So we we uh think that we're discovering a a pattern in the world but actually in believing in that pattern we are making the pattern where it it becomes a sort of self-fulfilling prophecy. The pattern wasn't there but by believing that it's there it becomes true. So in ideology, the basic idea is we the you promote the interests of the powerful by concealing true patterns with masking patterns that victims unwittingly work to reproduce at least partially. So you might think sort of the Christian um belief that the meek shall inherit the earth or God will redeem our suffering. these uh help make patterns um rather than track patterns that are there independently of these beliefs or uh example from Fuko it's only natural for men to desire women again it's pitched as a description of a pattern but it functions more to make a pattern true okay so I think active inference is a really nice way of making sense of a phenomena like ideology uh and that is because as a pointed out. Um what active inference says is that all that matters is that the discrepancy between a pred prediction or an expectation and an observation is minimized. But there are actually two ways that one can minimize uh these discrepancies. One can adjust the prediction so it better fits the observation or could one can adjust the world through action so that it better fit uh fits a prior prediction. Now that uh raises the following question um why don't we just adjust expectations always adjust our expectations in response to differences um discrepancies between extterceptive feedback and expectations. Um well the idea is that some expectations for example those related to fundamental requirements on survival like uh the your body temperature or levels of blood sugar are sticky and the only way to minimize the discrepancies with relevant feedback is to act. You can't adjust the level that your blood sugar should be at or the level that your body temperature should be at. The only way of minimizing the discrepancy is through action. So as Clark puts it, bringing about action requires attenuating, that is assigning low precision to the sensory information currently indexing the actual disposition of the body so as to enable precise propriceptive predictions to prevail. Intentional action thus depends upon a delicate balance that combines precise propriceptive predictions with attenuated information concerning current bodily states. An interesting consequence is that action thus depends on a kind of systematic misrepresentation of how our body is currently arrayed in space. I emphasize that because it's very similar to the idea of ideology. Ideology is a systematic misrepresentation of the way of the social world but it leads to a kind of alteration of the social world to more approximate it. So one of the interesting consequences of this picture is that we can replace this classic pattern finding and pattern making uh disjunction that we saw in SURL and Anskam, we could replace it um with a more nuanced um continuum. So here rather than two categories into which we could place thoughts or or judgments or sentences, we have a continuum that and anything you think or say can be placed on different places in this continuum depending on how sticky it is. That is how likely are we are to hold on to the belief no matter what and the prediction error that you get. So diet doesn't impact health. Presumably that's pretty we're willing to revise that and if the prediction error is high as it is in this case but something like life has meaning even if there's a lot of prediction error um we we can't help but hold on to it because it's very sticky and but it can vary for individual to individual or occasion to occasion and it's it's um a continuous variation not a discrete one like in the classical understanding of these things falling into one of two categories. So I think the talk I'm about to give is somewhat congress in the sense that uh you both discussed what it is that active inference allows us to do and and how it allows us to frame how we carve reality. But I think I'm about to talk about the mechanism at play when we talk about the ways in which our perception overlays with group dynamics and then um shapes that reality itself. Um and I'm going to go rather relatively quickly to give Andrew and Remy a chance to um talk as well. The core idea here is that every adaptive system biological or social operates by minimizing uncertainty. And so active inference starts with the individual. So the self model maintains coherence between internal states of the world, internal states and the world. Uh but because no one can model the entire world alone, cognition extends beyond just the individual. And so uh to reduce uncertainty we extend our cognitive boundaries by coupling our inferences with those of others. And the shared modeling of reality depends on uh shared prior. So overlapping assumptions about what it is likely uh meaningful or true. And so um trust here arises when we assume that others self-evidence as we do that they also act to maintain coherence between their predictions and the world. So through this mutual self-evidencing, individuals become part of each other's generative models, they enable social cognition, uh the the the ability to think together, to coordinate and to construct shared worlds. And so these distributed inference loops become the foundation of social identity and group belonging. And so self-esteem here functions as a precision regulator, a dynamic estimate of how well our internal model aligns with social feedback. So when prediction errors are low and feedback is affirming, confidence increases. And when errors rise and feedback turns negative, self-esteem decreases, prompting potentially self-correction. Um overconfidence and shame emerge when the system miscalibrates precision. So over confidence inflates certainty discounting error signals and preventing learning and shame amplifies precision on negative feedback overweighing error and suppressing action. So in this sense self-esteem is a sociometric gauge of prediction error keeping our self models coherent with the social world. And so through this continuous calibration, individuals align with collective expectations and they form more stable social groups. And so social groups can form around shared expectations or beliefs that collectively minimize uncertainty. And so over time, confirmation bias reinforces these shared priors. individuals selectively attend uh to evidence that supports their existing models and ignore what contradicts them. It's it's uh it links to what Tadu was saying about motivated reasoning. And so this feedback signalizes social identity but also traps the group in local minima um of of belief space the the states that feel coherent internally but are resistant to change. So within these echo chambers, prediction errors are reduced not by updating beliefs but by filtering experience itself and such stability preserves belonging at the cost of flexibility. So the system maintains low surprise through mutual reinforcement rather than genuine adaptation. And so once we have these shared beliefs, once they are stabilized, um power and influence can determine whose priors guide the collective models. Power here emerges when one agent or an ideology uh or ultimately a group can modulate the priors that guide collective sensemaking rather than controlling actions directly. Influence operates through the precision weighing of beliefs by determining what counts as credible, relevant or true. Leaders, narratives, institutions steer collective attention. They um shape how groups interpret evidence and update their models of the world. Under this view, power is the capacity to steer collective belief updating. It is to define the boundaries of what can be imagined or questioned. And so the group's generative model thus becomes a shared cognitive architecture governed by those who set its priors. And this hierarchical modulation scales from individuals and small groups to entire societies. But finally um we can express cultural, political and interpersonal processes in terms of shared inference. And we can formalize how meaning, trust, and coordination emerge without producing their complexity or richness. The goal here is not to necessarily mechanize society, but to make its hidden computations visible to describe how collective sense making evolves, adapts, and sometimes fails. And so we can use this to design systems that learn and align together. We can apply it to artificial intelligence but we can also apply it to policym large and that's the direction I hope to take here. >> Thank you. It was fascinating.
